# Per-video cards

One card per video, newest first (#1 is the newest upload). Numbers are measured by the scripts in `analysis/scripts/`; values marked *est.* come from separated audio stems or heuristic classifiers. Everything under **By eye** was written by one reviewer per video who looked at contact sheets, join sheets and frame montages (`analysis/review/REVIEW_PROMPT.md`); it is an observation, not a measurement, and was not cross-checked by a second reviewer. View, like and comment counts are the snapshot of 2026-11-23T18:24+00:00 UTC.

The first sentence of each video is quoted because the hook is the thing being studied. No other narration is reproduced; beat descriptions are paraphrases.

## 1 · Why Astronauts Can't Flush in Space

<https://www.youtube.com/shorts/NpRAk-5VKlo> · uploaded 2026-11-22 03:29 UTC · 61.3 s · 1080×1920, 30 fps

**Reach.** 593,228 views (rank 20 of 20) · 7,692 likes (1.3 per 100 views) · 223 comments (0.376 per 1,000 views) · 365,858 views per day over 1.6 days.

**Listing.** Description: “Why Astronauts Can't Flush in Space” (same as the title) · hashtags: none · tags: not readable (see `05_limitations.md`) · pinned comment: none among the fetched comments · creator comments among them: 0 · YouTube label: “Auto-dubbed” · sound credit: “Original Sound”.

**Hook.** “If you flush the toilet on a spaceship, you'll never make it back to Earth.” · 15 words, ends at 3.70 s · 12 words spoken in the first 3 s · form: if you + consequence.

**By eye: hook.** Frame 0: Smug boy in yellow vest with hands on hips beside a white toilet on a checkerboard floor, Earth in a porthole behind, toilet paper flying. First 3 s: Hero smirks then closes eyes for ~1.1s, hard cut at 1.17s to exterior window, then a ship above Earth tilts and fires its thruster. Title and first line: Title restates the topic as a question; the first line gives the dramatic consequence of the same act.

**By eye: story.** Topic: engineering/physics · era: future/space · structure: act first, explain at the end · likely source: invented parable · tense: mixed · second person: yes · direct-speech lines: 3 · explainer turn: 'But how' at 32.9s, then 'Well, a spaceship toilet' at 35.4s · ending: explanation is the last line · call to action: none.

Fact core as the reviewer understood it (not fact-checked): Spacecraft waste vented overboard freezes into glittering ice crystals and the vent gives tiny thrust, which could confuse star navigation.

Protagonist: Rookie astronaut in yellow vest who breaks the flush rule. Opposing figure: Mission Control and a shouting controller; crewmates in red and purple uniforms.

Tone: Absurd escalation from a toilet flush to being lost in space; exaggerated faces. Deadpan stakes with a rookie's smug eye-roll; faux-serious mission jargon. Dialogue: Short shouted lines (It's a toilet! Who flushed?) inside fast narration; no pauses.

| setup | 3.8 – 13.7 s | Mission Control schedules flushes; rookie ignores tonight's ban and scoffs at it. | Mission Control room, GO/NO GO screens, crew desks, rookie rolling eyes. |
| escalation | 13.7 – 23.4 s | At night he secretly flushes; morning, navigator sees hundreds of new stars. | Sleeping bags, hero at toilet, navigator in cockpit with floating sparkle stars. |

| ending | 52.0 – 61.2 s | Crew cannot tell fakes from real; without GPS they match real stars. | Crew in cockpit, phone searching, hero with telescope, scope LOCK, ship flies off. |

## 2 · The Prisoner Who Tunneled Into a Bank

<https://www.youtube.com/shorts/7dGWuCeZ8Ic> · uploaded 2026-11-20 02:03 UTC · 55.4 s · 1080×1920, 30 fps

**Reach.** 972,695 views (rank 18 of 20) · 18,662 likes (1.92 per 100 views) · 257 comments (0.264 per 1,000 views) · 264,205 views per day over 3.7 days.

**Listing.** Description: “The Prisoner Who Tunneled Into a Bank” (same as the title) · hashtags: none · tags: not readable (see `05_limitations.md`) · pinned comment: none among the fetched comments · creator comments among them: 0 · YouTube label: “Auto-dubbed” · sound credit: “Original Sound”.

**Hook.** “He dug a tunnel under his prison cell, yet it didn't lead out to freedom.” · 15 words, ends at 3.84 s · 12 words spoken in the first 3 s · form: he/they + past-tense action.

**By eye: hook.** Frame 0: Prisoner (orange jumpsuit, headlamp) stands in a bright cell, shovel raised at his side, gritted-teeth face to camera; small white-and-red X mark on floor; first caption word already on screen. First 3 s: Shovel swings down; from 0.8 s floor cracks into glowing orange hole with flying rubble; face swaps shocked then smug; hop with scale pop at 2.2 s; slow push-in; dissolve at 2.53 s to tunnel cutaway. Title and first line: Title compresses the premise (prisoner, tunnel, bank); first line gives prisoner and tunnel, the bank arrives in line two, so the title spoils part of the reveal.

**By eye: story.** Topic: crime/scam · era: modern · structure: act first, explain at the end · likely source: invented parable · tense: mixed · second person: no · direct-speech lines: 2 · explainer turn: 'You see' at 41.5 s · ending: explanation is the last line · call to action: none.

Fact core as the reviewer understood it (not fact-checked): Corrupt guards use a prisoner as free labour: they let him dig, skip his bunk at headcounts and smuggle the loot out in lunchboxes, splitting it with him.

Protagonist: Unnamed prisoner in orange jumpsuit with headlamp and brown hair tuft. Opposing figure: Prison guards, secretly in on the scheme; cellmate is a sarcastic foil.

Tone: Dry situational irony: a heist that cannot work, then guards running it; visual gags (donut guards, shades, plaid lunchbox) and the cellmate's sarcastic quip (estimate). Deadpan narration with a reversal; viewer is led to think the prisoner is foolish before learning the guards profit (dramatic irony); faces stay smug or blank. Dialogue: Two quoted one-liners embedded in the narration (cellmate's sarcastic congratulation, guard's one-word verdict); no separate character voices evident (audio not reviewed).

**Hook.** “He rolled the bomb into the enemy camp, but made sure it wouldn't go off.” · 15 words, ends at 3.70 s · 13 words spoken in the first 3 s · form: he/they + past-tense action.

**By eye: hook.** Frame 0: Night, long shot: teal-robed protagonist leans forward on a dirt road beside a glowing paper-lantern post, a small black bomb in his swung-back hand; caption 'He' low-centre. First 3 s: 0-0.83 s he rolls the bomb (head turns in 3D); hard cut at 0.83 s to a ground-level view behind a giant bomb rolling toward a moonlit bonfire camp while the camera creeps in; captions swap every 0.2-0.4 s. Title and first line: Title asks why samurai feared a named weapon; the first line instead opens on a paradox (a bomb rolled in but rigged not to explode) and the weapon's name only comes at about 42 s.

**By eye: story.** Topic: history/war · era: ancient/medieval · structure: act first, explain at the end · likely source: history · tense: mixed · second person: no · direct-speech lines: 2 · explainer turn: 'what's in there' at 41.4 s; the weapon is named at 42.5 s · ending: consequence shown, stops on impact · call to action: none.

Fact core as the reviewer understood it (not fact-checked): A long fuse coiled around a wooden core inside a sealed shell burns slowly, so a seeming dud can explode long after it lands.

Protagonist: Inventor-soldier in a teal robe who built the slow-fuse bomb. Opposing figure: His captain, then the besieging samurai army and their commander.

Tone: Light slapstick and irony: guards leap for cover from a dud, then treat it like treasure and bet on it; cartoon symbols (sweat drop, anger vein, star eyes) add gags. Dramatic irony: viewers know the fuse is burning while everyone calls it a dud; deadpan half-lidded faces, and two flat-faced survivors watch a mushroom cloud. Dialogue: Third-person narration; only two quoted lines, both the captain's; the speaking mouth flaps on those lines while other characters stay mute (estimate).

**By eye: hook.** Frame 0: Protagonist (red coat, white headband, bored half-lidded face) bent over a tall holed wooden board, hand-drilling; grey stone fort wall with a blue tiger banner behind, bright grass in front; caption 'He' at 70% height. First 3 s: 0-1 s slow push-in on the driller; 1-2 s fast swoop to the drill throwing wood chips into the holed board; 2-3 s whip up the wall past banner and pavilion to a wide valley with a tiny marching army. Title and first line: Title promises the outcome (a cart stops an army); the first line opens on an odd action (82 drilled holes) and withholds the cart, so the title's payoff stays hidden until the end.

**By eye: story.** Topic: history/war · era: ancient/medieval · structure: pure story with twist reveal · likely source: history · tense: mixed · second person: no · direct-speech lines: 2 · explainer turn: – · ending: consequence shown, stops on impact · call to action: none.

Fact core as the reviewer understood it (not fact-checked): A cart panel drilled with dozens of holes holds rocket arrows whose fuses are twisted into one rope, so one flame launches every rocket at once (hwacha-style launcher; estimate).

Protagonist: Deadpan engineer-soldier in a white headband and red coat who builds and guards the launcher cart.. Opposing figure: Plumed commander who mocks him; the yellow-uniformed attacking army..

Tone: Dry absurdity: a cheese-grater jibe from the commander, a man as a human umbrella, and an understated punchline over a mushroom cloud. Protagonist keeps a half-lidded deadpan face in nearly every shot; absurd actions are stated flatly; the smug arms-crossed pose before the explosion lands the irony. Dialogue: Only two quoted lines, both from side characters whose mouths animate; the protagonist never speaks; one narrator voices them (estimate, audio not heard).

Fact core as the reviewer understood it (not fact-checked): Baker-Miller pink (P-618, 1 pint red to 1 gallon white) is claimed to sap aggression for about 15 minutes, then backfire; as told in the video, not verified.

Protagonist: The scientist (brown hair, striped tie) who sets the paint recipe and the 15-minute rule; inmates are his test subjects.. Opposing figure: Prison guards who follow his rules; the pink cell itself flips from calming to enraging..

Tone: Light absurd gags: delighted guards with a doll-brand quip, a skinny guard beating a huge inmate in two seconds, a giant knitting a candy-striped scarf, then an over-the-top rage burst. Fast deadpan narration; dramatic irony in the reversal (a calming colour makes the calm man rage); a rhetorical 'wait, what' beat after the arm-wrestle. Dialogue: No on-screen speech: characters are mute with expression swaps; the one quoted guard remark is voiced inside the narration; captions mirror the narration only.

Fact core as the reviewer understood it (not fact-checked): Race results reached betting rooms by telegraph wire with a delay and bets stayed open until it arrived, so a faster signal (a mirror flash from the hill) lets someone bet on a race already decided.

Protagonist: Silent, smug bettor in a boater hat, mint vest and polka-dot bow tie (the 'mirror man'). Opposing figure: Mocking regulars (plaid-vest man in a derby, round-glasses man) and a pitying teller; no true villain.

Tone: Absurd exaggeration (a jockey waving at his mother, a horse so far back it is in the car park, a nag beaten by the dairy wagon it hauls) plus small visual gags (pretzel, bill on the teller's head). Deadpan narration with dramatic irony: the audience knows the dark hill matters while the room mocks him; the ending flips the room from laughing to stunned. Dialogue: Short taunts and one-liners from side characters plus a viewer-voiced question; faces and mouths animate while lines are spoken; no speech bubbles.

**By eye: story.** Topic: crime/scam · era: modern · structure: pure story with twist reveal · likely source: invented parable · tense: mixed · second person: no · direct-speech lines: 1 · explainer turn: "You see" at about 50.98 s · ending: twist reveal is the last line · call to action: none.

Fact core as the reviewer understood it (not fact-checked): Slow, tiny changes go unnoticed, so a former owner who kept a key can drive a family out and buy the house back cheap.

Protagonist: Dad in green sweater and round glasses. Opposing figure: Masked burglar, later revealed as the previous owner; a mocking police officer.

Tone: Dry absurdist: tiny shifts treated as a menace; slapstick poses, smug cat and officer. Strong deadpan; the inch-per-night premise delivered straight, twist reveals the burglar was the seller. Dialogue: One line of reported speech by the officer; rest is fast third-person narration, present tense then past.
